During the Iran–Iraq war of 1980–88, Iranian *Basiji* volunteered for martyrdom by marching across minefields to clear the way for the troops behind them.<sup>53</sup> (They were equipped with plastic keys before being sent to their deaths, to symbolize their entry directly into heaven.) Many of these suicidal mine-clearers were children, whose consent was irrelevant: their right to self-determination did not include the power to waive their right of self-ownership in this way.<sup>54</sup> And of the adults, no doubt some were manipulated or coerced in ways that compromised the validity of their consent. But what about the others—the wholehearted would-be martyrs? Surely, these suicidal men were acting in opposition to the ends they *should* have had; but there seems no reason to believe that they were all being made to serve ends that were not their own.

Of those men, we should deny that their right of self-ownership was violated. But this leaves open three other possible objections to taking advantage of their willingness to be used in this way.<sup>55</sup> One points to the obvious fact that they were being encouraged to do something that not only ended, but wasted, their lives: so the fact that this was bad for them was a strong reason not to treat them in that way, even if it violated no right of theirs.<sup>56</sup> A second is that their actions violently destroyed valuable human lives, which, like all precious things, call to be protected, not destroyed. And finally, there is this point. Suppose my commander knows nothing of my willingness to serve him, and does not care about that. He will get me to walk through the minefield whether I want to or not. Then his treatment of me still expresses his disrespect towards me, despite my consent. This is not just blameworthy, in not caring about my rights; it also actually treats me in an expressively disrespectful way. It is like the case of breaking a promise to someone who happens not to be inconvenienced by it.<sup>1</sup> He is treating me as though I did not have the right I have waived—as someone whose opinion about how I am to be treated does not matter. So his action is not just blameworthy, but wrong.<sup>57</sup>

As I see it, the position described in the two preceding paragraphs is consistent with Scanlon’s view that the permissibility of an action does not in general depend on the agent’s reasons for performing it, but that its meaning does, and that an action’s permissibility can sometimes be affected by its meaning (*Moral Dimensions*, pp. 4–6, 54–62). (On this topic, see also Victor Tadros, *The Ends of Harm*, pp. 155–60.)  
However, I do disagree with Scanlon’s treatment of cases of wrongful use as cases of *costly involvement without consent* (*Moral Dimensions*, Ch. 3, at p. 110). It is true that our reasons for not wanting to be subject to costly involvement without consent are prominent among the reasons for the existence of a right of self-ownership that protects us against being used as means to others’ ends. However, your use of me need not cost *me* anything to be a violation of my right. And when being used as a means is costly, one of the main costs is *being treated as someone who does not bear this right*.
43. This is consistent with accepting that, given the military context, there are other instrumental uses of soldiers which do not violate their rights—ordering one soldier to squat down so that another can stand on him and shoot over a parapet, for example. Moreover, it is an implication of my view that there could conceivably be an emergency great enough to provide a justification for infringing the right not to be force-marched across a minefield. Given a great enough catastrophe, I could be morally required to step on a mine to avert it. And if so, I could be morally permitted to make you step on a mine in my place when I am unable to do so myself, permissibly infringing your right. Indeed, a situation of the following kind is conceivable: there is an evil so great that we must organize ourselves into an army in which the front ranks walk over minefields to clear the way for the rest: there is no other way to resist it. Then I could have a *duty* to join that army and march in the front ranks, and I might possess no right against being made to do so.

49. Suppose a missile has been launched to detonate over a crowd, killing many people. Surely you could permissibly divert it to land at the front of the crowd, killing fewer people. But what if the reason it would kill fewer people is that the bodies of



the people at the front of the crowd would then absorb the blast, shielding the others? If I say that it is wrong to divert the trolley in the Loop Case, must I not say that it is wrong to divert the missile here?

I think that would be true if the situation with the crowd was relevantly similar to the Loop Case in three respects: (i) the 'crowd' contains only six people; (ii) five will be killed if the missile remains on its current trajectory, but one at the front will not be harmed; and (iii) each of the lives that is saved when the missile is diverted is only saved by using the one to shield them from the explosion. If those three features are present, then this case has the same structure as a Shield Case in which a shooter is aiming at five people, and all you can do to save them is to divert the shooter so that his line of fire is blocked by one person, whose body will act as a shield protecting the other five. The one, under those circumstances, has a right not to be used as a means of saving the five.

However, a more realistic case of diverting a missile to land in front of a crowd and kill fewer people will not have features (i)–(iii). Instead, the diversion will change the distribution of deaths from an outcome with pattern A to an outcome with pattern B. (Each cell represents a person in the crowd; 'x' indicates that the person is killed in that outcome.)

Here,  $x^{11}$  and  $x^{12}$  will die in either outcome. And in outcome B, part of the life-saving effect of the diversion will be due to the dissipating the force of the explosion into the ground, so that when the lives of  $x^{1-10}$  are saved they are not saved by using the people in front of them as shields. Therefore, although diverting the missile to produce outcome B results in the deaths of people whose lives were not already threatened, the saving of a greater number of lives has not been achieved by using those people as a means.

50. But nor do the five have a right against the one that he sacrifice his life to protect them: he is entitled (that is, has a Hohfeldian privilege) to escape, and I do not violate any rights of the five if I assist him. In this respect it is the same as a case in which there is only one person sheltering behind the fat man. It only becomes different if he is sheltering so many endangered people that he is morally required to sacrifice his own life to save them.
51. There are always variants. Suppose we change the trolley into a guided missile targeting the five, and the five have moved behind the fat man to shield themselves. Then I think they are violating his right not to be used as a means, and you are wrongly abetting the violation if you do not shout a warning.

55. These are objections to the view advanced by Arthur Flemming, 'Using a Man as a Means', p. 286, that '[a]s long as a man has been used by way of his unencumbered actions'—i.e. (i) he is a means by way of his voluntary action and (ii) he either knows of the purpose for which he is used or would still act as a means if he did know—'he has not been wronged'.
56. And if, in wrecking their lives, they were acting for the sake of a value that they would later have disowned, their case belongs to the same category of restrictions to the right to self-determination mentioned in note 54.
57. Putting this in Scanlon's terms, this is one of the cases in which the meaning of an action affects its permissibility (*Moral Dimensions*, pp. 5, 62). Notice the difference from Kant's shopkeeper, discussed in Section 7.1. His attitudes are indiscriminate, but in giving his customers the right change he does not wrong them. The contemptuous commander who discards the lives of his troops *does* something to them which is a form of mistreatment.
